t
,
(
36
)
at point
(0,m_{0})
.
Obviously, we can proceed in a similar manner when the initial time is some
t\in(0,T]

(it is easily checked that the sequences
(\epsilon_{N})_{N\geq 1}

and

(\eta_{N})_{N\geq 1}
do not depend on the choice of the initial condition, as we prescribed in the first step of the proof).

Fourth Step.

We now prove the second part of the statement.
The proof relies on
Remark

4.7
.
Combined with
Theorem

3.10
, it says
that, for any

t\in[0,T]
(replacing
0
by
t
in
Remark

4.7
)
and, for almost every

m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
the optimal trajectories issued from

(t,m)
satisfy the MFG system
(
5
)
with a prescribed initial value for the
backward equation (since the Fourier coefficients are uniquely determined).
By
[
14
, Proposition 2.1]
,
uniqueness follows at such points
(t,m)
.
∎

4.4.
Proof of the auxiliary lemmas

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Following the statement, we fix
N\geq 1

and we
take a point
m\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
such that the restriction of
V(0,\cdot)
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{N}
is differentiable
at
(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
.
Moreover, we call
(m_{t},u_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
a solution to the MFG system
that minimizes

{\mathcal{J}}_{\textrm{\rm det}}
.

The proof then relies on the principle of super-jets. We know that for another point
m^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
,

\begin{split}V(0,m^{\prime})&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(F(m_{t}^{\prime})+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}L\Bigl(x,-\partial_{p}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)dm_{t}^{\prime}(x)\Bigr)dt+G(m_{T}^{\prime}),\end{split}

for
(m_{t}^{\prime})_{0\leq t\leq T}
the solution of the Fokker-Planck equation

(45)

\partial_{t}m_{t}^{\prime}(x)-\textrm{div}\Bigl(\partial_{p}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\,m_{t}^{\prime}(x)\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\Delta m_{t}^{\prime}(x)=0,

with
m_{0}^{\prime}=m^{\prime}
as initial condition.

In turn,

\begin{split}V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(F(m_{t}^{\prime})-F(m_{t})+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}L\Bigl(x,-\partial_{p}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)d(m_{t}^{\prime}-m_{t})(x)\Bigr)dt\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+G(m_{T}^{\prime})-G(m_{T}),\end{split}

And then,
thanks to the fact that
F
and
G
have Lipschitz continuous derivatives,

\begin{split}&V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\frac{\delta F}{\delta m}(m_{t})(y)d(m_{t}^{\prime}-m_{t})(y)+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}L\Bigl(x,-\partial_{p}H\bigl(x,\nabla u_{t}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)d(m_{t}^{\prime}-m_{t})(x)\biggr\}dt\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\delta G}{\delta m}(m_{T})(y)d\bigl(m_{T}^{\prime}-m_{T}\bigr)(y)+C\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m_{t}^{\prime}(y)-m_{t}(y)|^{2}dy.\end{split}

Using the stability properties of
the linear equation
(
45
), the last term is less than
C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m^{\prime}(y)-m(y)|^{2}dy
(for a new value of
C
),
where we notice that
m
and
m^{\prime}
have a finite number of non-zero Fourier coefficients and thus have a smooth density.

We now replace
[{\delta G}/{\delta m}](m_{T})(y)
by
g(y,m_{T})=u_{T}(y)

and
[\delta F/\delta m](m_{t})(y)
by
f(y,m_{t})

and we use the MFG system (
5
)
and the probabilistic representation (
10
).
We get

(46)

\begin{split}V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)&\leq\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}u_{0}(y)d\bigl(m^{\prime}-m\bigr)(y)+C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m_{0}^{\prime}(y)-m_{0}(y)|^{2}dy.\end{split}

We deduce that
for

(\hat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{|F_{N}^{+}|}

\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}_{k}}V(m)\widehat{r}^{k}\leq\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{u}_{0}^{-k}\widehat{r}^{k}.

(Above, it is implicitly understood that
\widehat{r}^{k}=-\overline{\widehat{r}^{k}}
.)
And we should have equality by changing
r
into
-r
. This is sufficient to identify the real and imaginary parts of

\partial_{\widehat{m}_{k}}V(m)
and
\widehat{r}^{-k}
(consistently with the definition
(
30
)). This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.8
.

We observe that
\widehat{\mu}_{t}^{0}=1
, hence

\mu_{t}
has mass 1 for any
t\in[0,T]
. However,

\mu_{t}
may become negative valued for
t
away from
0
.
In order to prove that
\mu_{t}
stays positive for
t
close to
0
,
recall
the equation
(
16
) satisfied by

(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
Since the initial condition is Lipschitz continuous and bounded (as it is a density)
and since the drift
\alpha
therein is also Lipschitz continuous in space,
we can invoke standard estimates for parabolic equations to deduce that
the function
(t,x)\mapsto m_{t}(x)
is
1/2
-Hölder continuous in time and Lipschitz continuous in space
on
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
for some Hölder and Lipschitz constants only depending on
c
.
In particular, we can find
\epsilon_{N}(c)>0
only depending on
c
and
N
, such that

\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\bigl|\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}-\widehat{m}^{k}_{0}\bigr|\leq\frac{c}{2},

if
t\in[0,\epsilon_{N}(c)]
. And then, replacing

\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}
by
\widehat{\mu}_{t}^{k}
and recalling that

\widehat{m}_{0}^{k}=0
if
k\not\in F_{N}
,

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl|\mu_{t}(x)-m_{0}(x)\bigr|\leq\frac{c}{2},

which shows that

\mu_{t}
is positive
(and thus is a probability measure)
if
t\in[0,\epsilon_{N}(c)]
(since
m_{0}
is lower bounded by
c
).

Next, we consider a test function
h
that is 1-Lipschitz continuous. Then,
for
t\in[0,\epsilon_{N}(c)]
,

\begin{split}&\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}h(x)d\bigl(m_{t}-\mu_{t}\bigr)(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(h(x)-h*f_{N}(x)\bigr)d\bigl(m_{t}-\mu_{t}\bigr)(x),\end{split}

where we used the fact that
\widehat{h*f_{N}}^{k}=0
if
k\not\in F_{N}

and

\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}=\widehat{\mu}_{t}^{k}
if

k\in F_{N}
.
Now, since
h
is
1
-Lipschitz, there exists a sequence
(\eta_{N})_{N\geq 1}
converging to
0
(and independent of
h
in the class of
1
-Lipschitz continuous functions) such that

\|h-h*f_{N}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta_{N}
. We deduce that

(47)

d_{W_{1}}\bigl(m_{t},\mu_{t}\bigr)\leq\eta_{N}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl|m_{t}(x)-\mu_{t}(x)\bigr|^{2}dx\biggr)^{1/2}=\eta_{N}\biggl(\sum_{k\not\in F_{N}}\bigl|\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}\bigr|^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}.

Now,
we compute the dynamics of
\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}
for
k\not\in F_{N}
.
By integrating
(
16
)
with respect to
e_{k}
, we get

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}&=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}-\widehat{\textrm{\rm div}\bigl(\alpha_{t}m_{t}\bigr)}^{k}.\end{split}

Letting for simplicity
\beta_{t}=\textrm{\rm div}\bigl(\alpha_{t}m_{t}\bigr)
, we obtain, for
k\not\in F_{N}
(using the fact that
\widehat{m}_{0}^{k}=0
),

|\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|\widehat{\beta}^{k}_{s}|ds.

Recalling that
\alpha_{t}
and
m_{t}
are Lipschitz continuous in space (uniformly in
t
),
we deduce that there exists a constant
C(c)
such that

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}|\widehat{\beta}_{t}^{k}|^{2}\leq C(c).

Inserting in
(
47
),
we get
t\eta_{N}\sqrt{C(c)}
as upper bound for

d_{W_{1}}(m_{t},\mu_{t})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

We first prove that
V
is Lipschitz in space with respect to
d_{-2}
. Without any loss of generality, we can prove the Lipschitz property at time
t=0
.

By
(
46
)
in the proof of Lemma
4.6
, we know that, for any two
m,m^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
, for some
N\geq 1
,

\begin{split}V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)&\leq\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}u_{0}(y)d\bigl(m^{\prime}-m\bigr)(y)+C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m^{\prime}(y)-m(y)|^{2}dy,\end{split}

where
(m_{t},u_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a solution of the MFG system
(
5
) that minimizes
{\mathcal{J}}_{\textrm{\rm det}}
.
By Proposition

2.6
, the function
u_{0}
is
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
and we have a bound for
\|\nabla^{2}u_{0}\|_{\infty}

that only depends on the data in the assumption stated in
Subsection
2.1
. Therefore,

V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)\leq Cd_{-2}(m^{\prime},m)+C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m^{\prime}(y)-m(y)|^{2}dy,

with
C
being independent of
m
and
m^{\prime}
.

By choosing iteratively
(m,m^{\prime})
as
(m_{i},m_{i+1})
with

m_{i}=(1-i/n\bigr)m+(i/n)m^{\prime}
,
for
i\in\{0,\cdots,n-1\}
and for an integer
n\geq 1
,
we get

\begin{split}V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl[V(0,m_{i+1})-V(0,m_{i})\bigr]\\
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}Cd_{-2}(m_{i+1},m_{i})+C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m_{i+1}(y)-m_{i}(y)|^{2}dy\\
&=Cd_{-2}(m^{\prime},m)+\frac{C}{n}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|m^{\prime}(y)-m(y)|^{2}dy.\end{split}

Letting
n
tend to
\infty
, we get

V(0,m^{\prime})-V(0,m)\leq Cd_{-2}(m^{\prime},m).

The above is true when
m
and
m^{\prime}
are in
{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
. For any two general
m
and
m^{\prime}
, we can apply the above to

m*f_{N}
and
m^{\prime}*f_{N}
. We then observe that

\begin{split}d_{-2}\bigl(m^{\prime}*f_{N},m*f_{N}\bigr)&=\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{2}:\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\phi(x)\bigl(m^{\prime}*f_{N}-m*f_{N}\bigr)(x)dx\\
&=\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{2}:\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(\phi*f_{N}\bigr)(x)d\bigl(m^{\prime}-m\bigr)(x)\\
&\leq d_{-2}(m^{\prime},m).\end{split}

In the end, we have

V(0,m^{\prime}*f_{N})-V(0,m*f_{N})\leq Cd_{-2}(m^{\prime},m).

By Proposition

2.5
,
V
is continuous for the
1
-Wasserstein distance. We can easily pass to the limit in the left-hand side and then get that
V(0,\cdot)
is
C
-Lipschitz continuous for
d_{-2}
. The same argument can be applied at any time

t\in(0,T]
.

It now remains to prove that
V
is Lipschitz continuous in time. In order to do so, we use
the dynamic programming principle as follows.
Using the
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz property in space,
we know that, for any
t\in(0,T]

and an optimal control
(\alpha_{s})_{0\leq s\leq t}
(regarded as a bounded time-space feedback function),

\bigl|V(t,m_{t})-V(t,m_{0})|\leq Cd_{-2}(m_{t},m_{0})

where
(m_{s})_{0\leq s\leq t}
solves
(
16
)
with
m_{0}
as initial condition.

Now, using the form of the Fokker-Planck equation, we get

d_{-2}(m_{t},m_{0})=\sup_{\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{2}:\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\phi(x)d\bigl(m_{t}-m_{0}\bigr)(x)\leq Ct,

for a constant
C
that depends on
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}
, but, by
Proposition

2.6
, the latter can be assumed to be bounded
independently of the initial condition
m_{0}
.

It then remains to see from the dynamic programming principle
that (for a possibly new value of
C
)

\bigl|V(t,m_{t})-V(0,m_{0})\bigr|\leq Ct.

This completes the proof.
∎

4.5.
Mollification of
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz continuous and semi-concave generalized solutions

The aim of this subsection is to show that, provided that they are

d_{-2}
-Lipschitz continuous and semi-concave (in space),
generalized solutions can be mollified into functions that ‘nearly’ solve the original HJB
equation on the whole space (and not only almost everywhere). This is a key result to obtain uniqueness of

d_{-2}
-Lipschitz continuous and semi-concave generalized solutions.

For

W
a generalized solution of the HJB equation and for the same parameters
N
,
\varepsilon
and
\rho
as in Definition
3.13
, we call
the corresponding mollification

W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
(which implicitly depends on
\rho
, as emphasized in Definition

3.13
).
We start with the following observation:

Proposition 4.10
.

If
W:[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is

d_{-2}
-Lipschitz continuous in space, uniformly in time, then
there exists a constant
C
such that, for any
N\geq 1
,

\begin{split}&\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl|\partial_{x}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)\biggr|\leq C,\\
&\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl|\partial_{xx}^{2}\biggl[\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}\biggr)*f_{N}\biggr](x)\biggr|\leq C,\end{split}

at any point
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
at which the restriction
\widetilde{W}_{N}(t,\cdot)
of
W(t,\cdot)

to
{\mathcal{O}}_{N}
is differentiable.
Moreover, at those points,

\sum_{k\in F_{N}}|k|^{4}|\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)|^{2}\leq C.

Proof.

We recall that

{\mathcal{O}}_{N}
is an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}
.
Therefore,
for a point
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{N}

of differentiability of

\widetilde{W}_{N}
, we can choose

\varepsilon\in(0,1)
small enough such that, for any

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}
with

\widehat{r}^{-k}=\overline{\widehat{r}^{k}}

and
\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}|\widehat{r}^{k}|^{2}\leq 1
,

(\widehat{m}^{k}+\varepsilon\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
.
By the
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz property of
W
, we obtain

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}_{|\varepsilon=0}\widetilde{W}_{N}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{k}+\varepsilon\widehat{r}^{k}\bigr)_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\Bigr)\leq C\sup_{\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{r}^{k}\widehat{\phi}^{-k}.

for a constant
C
only depending on the
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz constant of

W
in space and whose value is allowed to vary from line to line.
And then,

\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{r}^{k}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)\leq C\sup_{\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{r}^{k}{\widehat{\phi}^{-k}}.

Obviously,
we can relax the condition

\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}|\widehat{r}^{k}|^{2}\leq 1

in the above inequality (as it is linear). In other words,
the above holds true for any

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}

with

\widehat{r}^{-k}=\overline{\widehat{r}^{k}}
for
k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
In particular, we can replace

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}

by

(k_{q}k_{q^{\prime}}\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}
, for some
q,q^{\prime}\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, from which we get

(48)

\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}k_{q}k_{q^{\prime}}\widehat{r}^{k}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)\leq C\sup_{\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{r}^{k}\bigl(k_{q}k_{q^{\prime}}\widehat{\phi}^{-k}\bigr).

The argument appearing in the supremum in the right-hand side can be rewritten (up to a multiplicative constant)

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}r(x)\partial^{2}_{x_{q}x_{q^{\prime}}}\phi(x)dx
with

r(x)=\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{r}^{k}e_{-k}(x)
.
Since the test function
\phi
satisfies

\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we get in the end

(49)

\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}k_{q}k_{q^{\prime}}\widehat{r}^{k}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|r(x)|dx.

This yields

(50)

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}r(x)\partial_{x_{q}x_{q^{\prime}}}^{2}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)dx\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|r(x)|dx.

We now use
(
50
)
in order to derive
the last
two claims in the statement.
In order to prove the second claim, we choose

r(x)=(s*f_{N})(x)
in
(
50
),
for an arbitrary function
s:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|s(x)|dx<\infty
. Since
f_{N}
is even, we get

\begin{split}&\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}s(x)\partial_{x_{q}x_{q^{\prime}}}^{2}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}*f_{N}(x)\biggr)dx\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}r(x)\partial_{x_{q}x_{q^{\prime}}}^{2}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)dx\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|r(x)|dx\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|s(x)|dx,\end{split}

which is exactly the second claim.

As for the last claim in the statement, it also follows
from (
50
),
by choosing therein

r(x)=\partial_{x_{q}x_{q^{\prime}}}^{2}(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x))
.

It then remains to derive the first claim in the statement. To do so, we rewrite
(
48
)–(
49
)
in the following manner.
Instead of using

(k_{q}k_{q^{\prime}}\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}

in
(
48
), we just use

(k_{q}\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}
, for some
q\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
.
Then, for

r(x)=\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\widehat{r}^{k}e_{-k}(x)
,
we reformulate
(
49
)
as

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}r(x)\partial_{x_{q}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)dx\leq C\sup_{\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}} r(x)\partial_{x_{q}}\phi(x)dx.

If we now take a square integrable function
s:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and
apply the above inequality to
r(x)=s_{N}(x)
, with
s_{N}(x)=\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\widehat{s}^{k}e_{-k}(x)
being the (cubic) Fourier sum of order
N
at
x
, then

\begin{split}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}s(x)\partial_{x_{q}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)dx&=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}s_{N}(x)\partial_{x_{q}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)dx\\
&\leq C\sup_{\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}} s_{N}(x)\partial_{x_{q}}\phi(x)dx\\
&=\sup_{\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}} s(x)\Bigl[\partial_{x_{q}}\phi\bigr]_{N}(x)dx,\end{split}

with

[\partial_{x_{q}}\phi]_{N}(x)=\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\widehat{\partial_{x_{q}}\phi}^{k}e_{-k}(x)
.
Here, a
d
-dimensional version of Dini theorem
(see
[
42
, Section 3]
,

[
67
, Corollary 2.1.2]

or

[
2
, Chapter 1, &2.2]
)
asserts that

\|[\partial_{x_{q}}\phi]_{N}-\partial_{x_{q}}\phi\|_{\infty}
converges to
0
as
N

tends to
\infty
, uniformly in

\phi
satisfying

\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. As a result, we write the above bound as

\begin{split}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}s(x)\partial_{x_{q}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m)e_{k}(x)\biggr)dx&\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}} |s(x)|dx.\end{split}

By an obvious density argument, we can easily pass from
s\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
to

s\in L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
in this inequality. This proves the first claim in the statement.
∎

We then have our main mollification result:

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
W
be
a
generalized solution of the HJB equation
that is

d_{-2}
-Lipschitz continuous and semi-concave in space, uniformly in time.
For any
c>1
,
there exist two families

(\eta_{N,\varepsilon})_{N,\varepsilon}

(of positive reals)
and

(R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})_{N,\varepsilon,\rho}

(of compactly supported bounded functions from
[0,T] \times{\mathbb{C}}^{|F_{N}^{+}|}
into
{\mathbb{R}}
, with the support and the bound depending on

N
and
\varepsilon
and not on
\rho
and
t
)
such that,
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,
for any
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
with

\|\nabla m\|_{\infty}\leq c
and
m\geq 1/c
,

\begin{split}&\biggl|\partial_{t}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\bigl(x,\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)\bigr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(x)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\textrm{\rm Trace}\Bigl[\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)\Bigr]dm(x)+F^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(m)\biggr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr),\end{split}

with

\lim_{(N,\varepsilon)\rightarrow(\infty,0)}\eta_{N,\varepsilon}=0
,
and, for any
t,N,\varepsilon
fixed,

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow\delta_{0}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)\Bigr|\bigotimes_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}d\Bigl(\Re[\widehat{m}^{k}],\Im[\widehat{m}^{k}]\Bigr)=0.

Above, the convergence
\rho\rightarrow\delta_{0}
is understood as
the weak convergence of

\rho
to
\delta_{0}
, the Dirac point mass at
0
(in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
).

The reader will notice the difference between
Definition

4.1

and
Proposition

4.11
: not only the main inequality
in
Proposition
4.11

is stated
everywhere in space, but
also
the
derivatives
are derivatives on the space of probability measures (as opposed to
the derivatives in
Definition

4.1
, which are just finite dimensional).
This is more consistent with the original formulation of the HJB equation,
see (
22
). See also
Remark

4.3
for a related comment.

Proof.

Throughout the proof,
the constant
c>1
is kept fixed.
We recall the
following
notation from
Definition

4.1
:

A_{N}(c)=[0,T]\times B_{N}(c)
with
B_{N}(c):={\mathcal{P}}_{N}\cap B(c)
and
B(c):=\{m:\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla m(x)|\leq c,\ \inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}m(x)\geq 1/c\}
.

First Step.

From the Definition

4.1

of a generalized solution,
there exists a full subset

\widetilde{A}_{N}(c)
of
A_{N}(c)

(for the image of the Lebesgue measure by the mapping
(t,(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\mapsto(t,{\mathscr{I}}_{N}((\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}}))
, see Proposition
3.8
) such that

\begin{split}&\biggl|\partial_{t}W(t,m*f_{N})-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m*f_{N})e_{k}(y)\biggr)d(m*f_{N})(y)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m*f_{N})\widehat{m}^{k}\widehat{f_{N}}^{k}+F(m*f_{N})\biggr|\leq\eta_{N}(c),\end{split}

when
(t,m*f_{N})\in\widetilde{A}_{N}(c)
. Above, we specified the dependence of the sequence
(\eta_{N})_{N\geq 1}
upon the constant
c
by writing

(\eta_{N}(c))_{N\geq 1}
.

Fix now

m\in B(c)
, that is
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
with

\|\nabla m\|_{\infty}\leq c
and
m\geq 1/c
.
Fix also

t\in[0,T]
such that

\{m^{\prime}:(t,m^{\prime})\in\widetilde{A}_{N}(2c)\}
is a full subset
of
({\mathcal{P}}_{N}\cap\{m^{\prime}:\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla m^{\prime}(x)|\leq 2c,\ \inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}m^{\prime}(x)\geq 1/(2c)\})
: by Fubini’s theorem, the set of such
t
’s is a full subset of
[0,T]
.
By definition of

\delta_{N,\varepsilon}

(which we merely denote by
\delta
in the rest of the proof)
in
Definition

3.12
, we observe that,
for

r=(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
such that
|\widehat{r}^{k}|\leq\delta
,
the probability measure

m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)
(as given by Definition

3.13
)
satisfies

\|\nabla m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}\leq 2c
and

m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)\geq 1/(2c)
for

\varepsilon
small enough (independently of
N
).
Therefore,

(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N})
belongs to

A_{N}(2c)
.
Since the image of the Lebesgue measure
by the mapping

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\mapsto m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
is obviously absolutely-continuous, we deduce that

(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N})\in\widetilde{A}_{N}(2c)
, for almost every

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
satisfying

|\widehat{r}^{k}|\leq\delta

for all
k\in F_{N}^{+}
.
Hence, we get, for almost every

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
,

\displaystyle\biggl|\partial_{t}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)d\bigl(m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)(y)

(51)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt-\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)}^{k}\widehat{f_{N}}^{k}+F\bigl(m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\biggr|\leq\eta_{N}(2c).

Second Step.

The conclusion
(
51
)
of the first step right above holds true for almost every
t\in[0,T]
, for any

m\in B(c)
, and for
almost every

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
satisfying

|\widehat{r}^{k}|\leq\delta

for all
k\in F_{N}^{+}
.

Therefore, integrating
(
51
)
over
(\Re[\widehat{r}^{k}],\Im[\widehat{r}^{k}])_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
with respect to
\otimes_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho
and recalling
Corollary

3.16
(for the existence of the time-derivative below), we obtain, for almost every
t\in[0,T]

and for any
m\in B(c)
,

(52)

\Bigl|\partial_{t}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)+F^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(m)-T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)-T_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\Bigr|\leq\eta_{N}(2c),

with

\begin{split}&T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\\
&:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)d\bigl(m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\biggr\}\\
&\hskip 300.0pt\times\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j},\end{split}

and

T_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m):=\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)}^{k}\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\widehat{f}_{N}^{k}.

We recall from
Proposition

3.14

(see also Corollary

3.16
)
that,
for any
(t,m)\in[0,T]\times B_{N}(c)
,

(53)

\begin{split}&\frac{\delta W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}}{\delta m}(t,m)(x)\\
&=(1-\varepsilon)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}\Bigr)*f_{N}\Bigr](x)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\\
&=(1-\varepsilon)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\Bigl[\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\hat{f}_{N}^{k}e_{k}(x)\Bigr]\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j},\end{split}

where we used on the last line the fact that

e_{k}*f_{N}(x)=e_{k}(x)\widehat{f}_{N}^{-k}=e_{k}(x)\widehat{f}_{N}^{k}
. We deduce that

(54)

\hbox{\hskip 476.73471pt\hskip-41.36247pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\biggl\{\frac{\delta W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}}{\delta m}(t,m)\biggr\}$}\hskip-41.36247pt\hskip-476.73471pt\raisebox{19.41238pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{56952404}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-476.73471pt\hskip 476.73471pt}^{-k}=(1-\varepsilon)\hat{f}^{k}_{N}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\hat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j},

for
k\in F_{N}
.

Third Step.

The main difficulty of the proof is to handle
T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)
.
The goal is to identify it with the second term in the main inequality of the statement. In order
to do so,
we first observe that this latter term can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\Bigl(y,\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)

(55)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\hbox{\hskip 476.73471pt\hskip-41.36247pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\biggl\{\frac{\delta W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}}{\delta m}(t,m)\biggr\}$}\hskip-41.36247pt\hskip-476.73471pt\raisebox{19.41238pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{56952404}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-476.73471pt\hskip 476.73471pt}^{k}e_{-k}(y)\biggr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 350.0pt\times d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y),

which prompts us to let

\begin{split}&T_{3}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m):=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\\
&\hskip 350.0pt\times d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y).\end{split}

(Pay attention that there is no
1-\varepsilon
and no

\widehat{f}_{N}^{k}

inside
H
in the above right-hand side.)
By the local Lipschitz property of
H
, we can find a constant
C
, only depending on the parameters in
the assumptions, such that

\begin{split}&\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\Bigl(y,\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)-T_{3}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\biggr|\\
&\leq C\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl\{\biggl|\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\biggl|\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|\biggr\}^{2}d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\biggr]^{1/2}\\
&\hskip 5.0pt\times\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl|\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\bigl((1-\varepsilon)\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}-1\bigr)k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|^{2}\\
&\hskip 250.0pt\times d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\biggr]^{1/2}.\end{split}

We now show that the term on the last two lines above tends to
0

as
(N,\varepsilon)
tends to
0
. The same strategy would permit to show that the term on
the first two lines of the right-hand side is bounded by a constant
C(c)
depending on
the parameters in the assumptions and on the value of
c
.
In order to proceed, we observe that
m
and
m*f_{N}
are bounded by
c+1
since
m\in B_{N}(c)
.
Therefore, allowing the constant
C
to depend on
c
and thus writing
C(c)
instead of

C
, we get

\begin{split}&\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl|\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\bigl((1-\varepsilon)\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}-1\bigr)k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|^{2}\\
&\hskip 250.0pt\times d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\\
&\leq C(c)\sum_{k\in F_{N}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|k|^{2}\bigl|(1-\varepsilon)\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}-1\bigr|^{2}\Bigl|\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\Bigr|^{2}\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}dy.\end{split}

We now use the following three facts: First,
|\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}|\leq 1
;
Second,
\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}
tends to
1
when
N
tends to
\infty
and
k
is fixed;
Third,
Proposition
4.10

gives a bound for
the series
\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}|k|^{4}|\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N})|^{2}
(at points where the derivatives exist).
Then, for a fixed integer
N_{0}
, we can separate the sum in the above right-hand side
depending whether
|k|\leq N_{0}
or

|k|>N_{0}
.
For
|k|\leq N_{0}
, we can easily use the fact that

(1-\varepsilon)\widehat{f}_{N}^{k}
tends to
1
as
(N,\varepsilon)
tends to
(\infty,0)
.
For
|k|\geq N_{0}
, we can easily bound

|k|^{2}
by
|k|^{4}/N_{0}^{2}
. All in all, this proves that the right-hand side
tends to
0
as
(N,\varepsilon)
tends to
(\infty,0)
. We finally have

(56)

\begin{split}&\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\Bigl(y,\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)-T_{3}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\biggr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c),\end{split}

where
\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c)
is a generic notation for a sequence that may depend on
c
(but not on

(t,m)
) and that tends to
0
as

(N,\varepsilon)
tends to
(\infty,0)
.

Fourth Step.

We now let

\displaystyle\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)

(57)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt-H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}}\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\biggr\}d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y).

Using the notation introduced in the third step, this is also equal to

\begin{split}\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)&:=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr\}\\
&\hskip 300.0pt\times d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-T_{3}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m).\end{split}

By convexity of the Hamiltonian,

\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)
is obviously non-negative.
Moreover, in
(
57
),
we can regard each
\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(0)*f_{N})

as a (almost-everywhere well-defined) function of vectors
(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{|F_{N}^{+}|}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}

such that

(\widehat{m}^{k}\widehat{f}_{N}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{N}
: in order to clarify the notation, we write
it in the form
\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}(t,(\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}})
, with
\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}(t,\cdot)
being seen as an element of
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|})

that is equal to
0
at
vectors

(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}

such that

(\widehat{m}^{k}\widehat{f}_{N}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\not\in{\mathcal{O}}_{N}
.
Then, we rewrite
(
57
)
in the form

(58)

\displaystyle\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}+\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt-H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}+\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)e_{k}(y)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\biggr\}\widehat{m}^{\ell}\widehat{f}^{\ell}_{N}e_{-\ell}(y)dy.

Notice that we expanded the convolution product
m*f_{N}
in Fourier coefficients. This is a way to emphasize the fact that

\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,\cdot)
can be here regarded as a real-valued function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}
.
To make the identification clear, we can denote the right-hand side in the above equality
by
\widetilde{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,(\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}})
, with

\widetilde{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
. We then have

\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)=\widetilde{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,(\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}})
.
We now make the following observation. If
\rho(\widehat{r}^{j})d\widehat{r}^{j}
were formally set equal to
\delta_{0}(d\widehat{r}^{j})

and if

\beta^{N,\varepsilon,k}
were continuous in the second argument,
the term on the right-hand side
would be equal to 0, hence leaving us with

\widetilde{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\delta_{0}}\bigl(t,(\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\bigr)=0,\quad(\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|},

where the symbol
\delta_{0}
in the left-hand side is for the Dirac mass at
0
and is to indicate that
\rho\equiv\delta_{0}
.
In turn, if

\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}
were continuous in the second argument,
the right-hand side in
(
58
) would converge to
0

as
\rho
tends to
\delta_{0}
.
Obviously,

\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}
is not continuous in our setting. However,
by
standard mollification results in finite dimension, we can approximate (for any
t
) the functions

(\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}(t,\cdot))_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}

by continuous functions
in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|})
.
For
(N,\varepsilon,k)
and
t
fixed, we can indeed
find
a collection of real-valued continuous functions

(\check{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)-\check{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k,n}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)\Bigr|\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{m}^{j}=0.

Since each

\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}(t,\cdot)

is bounded and zero outside
\{(\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{|F_{N}^{+}|}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}:(\widehat{m}^{j}\widehat{f}_{N}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{N}\}
, we can assume that the functions

(\check{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

are uniformly bounded and are zero outside a common bounded subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}
.
Denoting by

\check{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho,n}((\widehat{m}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}})
the right-hand side in
(
58
)
when
\widetilde{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k}
therein is replaced by

\check{\beta}^{N,\varepsilon,k,n}

and using the local Lipschitz property of

H
, we deduce that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\widetilde{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)-\check{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho,n}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)\biggr|\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{m}^{j}=0,

the convergence being uniform with respect to
\rho
satisfying the prescription
of Definitions

3.12

and

3.13
.
Using the fact that, for each fixed
n
,

\check{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho,n}

converges to
0
in sup norm as
\rho
tends to
\delta_{0}
, we
deduce that

(59)

\begin{split}\lim_{\rho\rightarrow\delta_{0}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\widetilde{\psi}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{j}\bigr)_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)\Bigr|\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{m}^{j}=0.\end{split}

Of course, the rate in the above convergence depends on
N

and
\varepsilon
.
Importantly,
for
N
and
\varepsilon
fixed,
the function

\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,\cdot)

is bounded uniformly in
\rho
and
t
: this follows from
Proposition

4.10
.
In short, this provides the function
R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
in the statement.

Fifth Step.

By combining the third and fourth steps,
we now rewrite
(
56
)
in the following form:

(60)

\begin{split}&\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\Bigl(y,\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)+\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\biggr\}\\
&\hskip 300.0pt\times\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|\\
&\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c).\end{split}

Back to the second step, we can compare the term on the second line with
T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
introduced in the second step.
The main difference between the two comes from the
measure that is used for integrating in
y
. Here, it is

m*f_{N}
, while it is

m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}
in

T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
.
In order to pass from one term to the other, we recall from the choice of
\delta

in
Definition

3.12

that

\bigl\|m-m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq C(c)\varepsilon,

when

r=(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
is in the support of
\bigotimes_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho

and
for
C(c)
depending on
c
.
Therefore,

(61)

\bigl\|m*f_{N}-m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq C(c)\varepsilon.

By using once again
Proposition

4.10

in order to bound the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the function

y\mapsto{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N})e_{k}(y)

and
by invoking
the local Lipschitz property of
H
,
we have

\begin{split}&\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\biggr\}\\
&\hskip 300.0pt\times\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\biggl\{\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\biggl(y,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\sum_{k\in F_{N}}k\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{k}(y)\biggr)d\bigl(m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)(y)\biggr\}\\
&\hskip 300.0pt\times\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|\\
&\leq C(c)\varepsilon.\end{split}

As we already mentioned,
the second term in the left-hand side is nothing but

T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)
.
Therefore,
(
60
)
yields

(62)

\begin{split}&\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}H\Bigl(y,\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr)d\bigl(m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)(y)+\psi^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)-T_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\biggr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c).\end{split}

Sixth Step.

We now address the term
T_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
defined in the second step.
We recall

T_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)=\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}\biggl(\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)}^{k}\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr).

Back to the analysis of

T[m,W]
in
Subsection

4.1
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr]\Delta m(y)dy=-\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\widehat{m}^{k}.\end{split}

Using Equation (
53
), we get

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr]\Delta m(y)dy\\
&=-(1-\varepsilon)\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\hat{m}_{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\hat{f}^{k}_{N}\widehat{m}^{k}\biggr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}.\end{split}

We then proceed as in the second step in order to remove the product
(1-\varepsilon)\widehat{f}^{k}_{N}
from the summand in the right-hand side.
In short,
we observe that the
family of Fourier coefficients

(\widehat{m}^{k})_{k}
is square-integrable, uniformly in

m\in B(c)
(see
(
35
)).
Moreover,
by Proposition

4.10
,
the sum

\sum_{k\in F_{N}}|k|^{4}|\partial_{\hat{m}^{k}}W(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N})|^{2}
is finite, uniformly in

N
,

\varepsilon
,

m
and
r
.
Therefore, we deduce that
(as before, for any
t\in[0,T]
and any
m\in B(c)
)

\begin{split}&\biggl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr]\Delta m(y)dy\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+(1-\varepsilon)\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{m}^{k}\biggr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c),\end{split}

which gives

\begin{split}&\biggl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr]\Delta m(y)dy\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}}\biggl(\sum_{k\in F_{N}}2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{m^{N,\varepsilon}(0)}^{k}\biggr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c).\end{split}

Recall now that, from our choice of
\delta_{N,\varepsilon}

in
Definition

3.12
,

\sum_{k\in|F_{N}|}|k|^{2}\bigl|\widehat{m^{N,\varepsilon}(0)}^{k}-\widehat{m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)}^{k}\bigr|\leq\varepsilon,

when

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}
is in the support of
\bigotimes_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho
.
We deduce that

\begin{split}&\biggl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\textrm{\rm Trace}\Bigl[\partial_{y}\partial_{\mu}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr]m(y)dy+T_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\biggr|\\
&=\biggl|\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}W^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(y)\Bigr]\Delta m(y)dy+T_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)\biggr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}(c).\end{split}

Together
with
(
52
),
(
59
)
and
(
60
), this completes the proof.
∎

4.6.
Uniqueness of Lipschitz, semi-concave admissible generalized solutions

Here is now the main statement of this section:

Theorem 4.12
.

There is a unique
generalized solution to the HJB equation (
22
) that is

d_{-2}
-Lipschitz and semi-concave in space uniformly in time.

Proof.

Main lines.

We use the notation
from Definition

4.1
, namely

A_{N}(c)=[0,T]\times B_{N}(c)
with
B_{N}(c)={\mathcal{P}}_{N}\cap B(c)
and

B(c):=\{m:\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla m(x)|\leq c,\ \inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}m(x)\geq 1/c\}
, for
c\geq 1
.

Given two solutions
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
of (
22
)
that are
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz and semi-concave in space,
uniformly in time,
we let

(63)

\begin{split}&D{\mathcal{H}}_{\lambda}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x):=\partial_{p}H\Bigl(x,\lambda\partial_{\mu}V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)+(1-\lambda)\partial_{\mu}V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)\Bigr),\\
&D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x):=\int_{0}^{1}D{\mathcal{H}}_{\lambda}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)d\lambda.\end{split}

For any
c\geq 1
,
there exist two families
(\eta_{N,\varepsilon})_{N,\varepsilon}
and

(R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})_{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
as in
Proposition

4.11
such that, for almost every
t\in[0,T]
, for any

m\in B(c)
,

\displaystyle\biggl|\partial_{t}\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr)(t,m)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\textrm{\rm Trace}\Bigl[\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr)(t,m)(x)\Bigr]dm(x)

(64)

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)\cdot\partial_{\mu}\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\big)(t,m)(x)d\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(x)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\widehat{m}^{k}\bigr)_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr).

The very idea of the proof is to consider the characteristics associated with the differential operator appearing in the left-hand side.
Strictly speaking, this should be a (non-linear) Fokker-Planck equation driven by the field

(t,m,x)\mapsto D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)
.
However, this is not what
we use next.
Because of our definition of a generalized solution, which is based on a truncation
of the Fourier expansion of the measure argument
m
, we switch to ‘approximate characteristics’, given by the Fokker-Planck equation:

(65)

\partial_{t}m_{t}(x)-\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m_{t})(x)\bigl(m_{t}*f_{N}\bigr)(x)\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\Delta m_{t}(x)=0,

with an initial condition
m_{0}
such that
\widehat{m}_{0}^{k}=0
for
|k|\geq N
, for some integer
N
,
and
m_{0}\geq 1/c
for some
c\geq 1
.
Importantly, the Fokker-Planck equation has a solution that is
a probability measure
for
N

large enough
(whilst the total mass is obviously preserved by the dynamics, the sign may not be).
Even more,
we can find a constant
c^{\prime}\geq 1
(depending on
c
) such that, for
N
large enough,
the solution

(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

is lower bounded by
1/c^{\prime}
and its gradient (in space) is bounded by
c^{\prime}
, see
Theorem
6.15
.
The thrust of
(
65
)
is that the
equation
satisfied by the Fourier coefficients
(\widehat{m}_{t}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}

is closed (meaning that it does not depend on the higher Fourier modes

(\widehat{m}_{t}^{k})_{k\not\in F_{N}^{+}}
): intuitively,
equation (
65
)
is ‘almost’ a finite dimensional dimensional ordinary differential equation.

Once
(
65
)
has been solved, we want to plug

(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
into
(
64
)
and then expand

((V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})(t,m_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
The very main point of the proof is then to show that, whenever

N
and
\varepsilon
are fixed,
the remainder

(66)

\Bigl(R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigl(t,(\widehat{m}_{t}^{j})_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\bigr)\Bigr)_{0\leq t\leq T}

tends to
0
when
\rho
tends to the Dirac mass
\delta_{0}
(compare if needed with the statement of
Proposition

4.11
).
This is however a challenging fact because

(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

itself
depends on
\rho

(although the notation does not make it clear).
And, even more, the reader must recall that, although

R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
is known to tend to
0
as
\rho
tends to
\delta_{0}
(when

N
and
\varepsilon
are fixed), the convergence just holds true in
L^{1}
(once again,
we refer to the statement of
Proposition

4.11
).
Some work is thus needed to adapt the result of
Proposition

4.11

to
(
66
).

Roughly speaking, the idea is to prove that the flow generated by the Fokker-Planck equation
(
65
)
cannot accumulate mass: If, instead of
m_{0}
,
the initial condition
of
(
65
)
is taken as a small random perturbation of
m_{0}
in
B_{N}(c)
,
with the perturbation having a bounded density with respect to the Lebesgue measure
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}
, then the law of

(\widehat{m}_{t}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}

with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}

also has a bounded density, the bound depending on
N
and

\varepsilon
, but not on
t
and
\rho
.
This claim is
stated in the form of Lemma

4.13
below, which we use in the rest of this proof and which we prove separately.

Application.

Following our plan, we take an initial condition

m_{0}\in B_{N}(c)
.
For

(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}
, we let

m_{0}(r)
be the element of
{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
defined by:

\widehat{m_{0}(r)}^{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widehat{m}_{0}^{k}+\widehat{r}^{k},&k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\},\\
0,&k\not\in F_{N},\end{array}\right.

with the usual convention that
\widehat{r}^{-k}=\overline{\widehat{r}^{k}}
.
If we choose
(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}

such that

(67)

\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}|k||\widehat{r}^{k}|<\frac{1}{2c},

then
m_{0}(r)\in B_{N}(2c)
and we can still solve the Fokker-Planck equation
(
65
)
with
m_{0}(r)
as initial condition
provided that
N\geq N_{c}
for some threshold
N_{c}
only depending on
c
, on the regularity properties of
H
in the variables
x
and
p
and on the
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz constant of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, see Theorem

6.15

in the appendix. We call the solution

(m_{t}(r))_{0\leq t\leq T}
:
Theorem

6.15

asserts that
(m_{t}(r))_{0\leq t\leq T}
forms a flow of probability measures.
Even more,
Theorem

6.15

also says that
there exists
c^{\prime}\geq 1
, independent of
N
,
\varepsilon
and
\rho
(but depending on
c
), such that
each

m_{t}(r)
belongs to
B(c^{\prime})
.

We now expand
((V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})(t,m_{t}(r)))_{0\leq t\leq T}

by means of the chain rule for
real-valued functions defined on

[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
Although this chain rule is well documented (see for instance
[
25
, Chapter 5]
), it here requires
a modicum of care since the time derivative
of

(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})

is just known to exist for almost every
t
, for all
m
, see
Corollary

3.16
.
The proof is as follows.
If
(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})
were smooth, then

[
25
, Chapter 5]

would yield

(68)

\begin{split}&\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(T,m_{T}(r)\bigr)=\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(0,m_{0}(r)\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{0}^{T}\biggl\{\partial_{t}\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(t,m_{t}(r)\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigl(t,m_{t}(r)\bigr)(x)\cdot\partial_{\mu}\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(t,m_{t}(r)\bigr)(x)d\bigl[m_{t}(r)\bigr](x)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\textrm{\rm Trace}\Bigl[\partial_{x}\partial_{\mu}\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(t,m_{t}(r)\bigr)(x)\Bigr]d[m_{t}(r)](x)\biggr\}dt.\end{split}

Under our assumption, we can apply
the above chain rule
to
a mollified version of
(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho})
,
for instance

\int_{\mathbb{R}}[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}](t-s,m)\varsigma(s)ds
,
for a smooth compactly supported density

\varsigma
on
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The most difficult term to handle in the resulting form of
(
68
) is

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(t-s,m_{t}(r)\bigr)\varsigma(s)dsdt.

By
Corollary

3.16
, for almost every

t\in[0,T]
, the function

(t,m)\mapsto\partial_{t}[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}](t,m)
is continuous in
m
, with a modulus of continuity independent of
t
. Therefore we can easily approximate the argument

m_{t}(r)
appearing in the integrand
by

\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\mathbf{1}}_{[t_{i-1},t_{i})}(t)m_{t_{i}}(r)

for a finite subdivision

t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}=T

of
[0,T]
with an arbitrarily small step. Using this approximation, it is pretty easy to prove that

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(t-s,m_{t}(r)\bigr)\varsigma(s)dsdt\rightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{t}\bigl[V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr]\bigl(t,m_{t}(r)\bigr)\varsigma(s)dt,

as
\varsigma
tends to
\delta_{0}
in the weak sense.
As a corollary, we deduce that
(
68
)
holds true under the standing assumption.
Since
V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
and
V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
coincide at time

T
, we deduce from
(
64
) that

\begin{split}&\Bigl|\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr)\bigl(0,m_{0}(r)\bigr)\Bigr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{T}R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-17.43408pt\hbox{$\bigl\{m_{t}(r)\bigr\}$}\hskip-17.43408pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{k}\bigr)_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)dt.\end{split}

For
\rho_{0}
a kernel similar to
\rho
, with a radius (compare if needed with
Definition

3.12
, the choice of the radius being here adapted to the constraint
(
67
)) less than
1/(2c\sqrt{dN}|F_{N}|)
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr)\bigl(0,m_{0}(r)\bigr)\Bigr|\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho_{0}\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{T}\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\Bigl(t,\bigl(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-17.43408pt\hbox{$\bigl\{m_{t}(r)\bigr\}$}\hskip-17.43408pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{18.50012pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{k}\bigr)_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigr)\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho_{0}\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr]dt.\end{split}

Here is now the
place where we invoke Lemma

4.13
(whose statement is given next): For any
t\in[0,T]
, we
perform the change of variable

y=m_{t}(r)
, i.e.

\widehat{y}^{k}=\widehat{m_{t}(r)}^{k}
, for

k\in F_{N}^{+}
. As a corollary of
Lemma
4.13
, we deduce that there exists a constant
C_{N,\rho_{0}}
, depending on
N
and

\rho_{0}
, but independent of
\varepsilon
and
\rho
,
such that

(69)

\begin{split}&\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr)\bigl(0,m_{0}(r)\bigr)\Bigr|\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho_{0}\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+C_{N,\rho_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigl(t,(\widehat{y}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{y}^{j}\biggr]dt.\end{split}

From the standing assumption,
we know
that

V_{1}

and

V_{2}

are Lipschitz continuous with respect to
d_{-2}
and thus with respect to
d_{W_{1}}
.
Therefore,
by a straightforward adaptation of the proof of Lemma
3.15
,

V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(0,\cdot)
(resp.
V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(0,\cdot)
)
converges uniformly to

V_{1}(0,\cdot)
(resp.
V_{2}(0,\cdot)
) as

(N,\varepsilon)
tends to
(\infty,0)
.
Hence,

\begin{split}&\lim_{(N,\varepsilon)\rightarrow(\infty,0)}\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\bigl(V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}-V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigr)\bigl(0,m_{0}(r)\bigr)\Bigr|\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho_{0}(\widehat{r}^{j})\otimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\\
&-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}|(V_{1}-V_{2})(0,m_{0}(r))|\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho_{0}(\widehat{r}^{j})\otimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr|=0.\end{split}

Therefore,
(
69
)
can be rewritten in the following form:

\begin{split}&\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\Bigl|\bigl(V_{1}-V_{2}\bigr)\bigl(0,m_{0}(r)\bigr)\Bigr|\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho_{0}\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+C_{N,\rho_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigl(t,(\widehat{y}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{y}^{j}\biggr]dt,\end{split}

for a possibly new value of the family
(\eta_{N,\varepsilon})_{N,\varepsilon}
, which still satisfies

\lim_{(N,\varepsilon)\rightarrow(\infty,0)}\eta_{N,\varepsilon}=0
, and then,
using the form of
\rho_{0}
and
following the proof of Lemma

3.15
,

(70)

\begin{split}&\Bigl|\bigl(V_{1}-V_{2}\bigr)\bigl(0,m_{0}\bigr)\Bigr|\leq\eta_{N,\varepsilon}+C_{N,\rho_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}R^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigl(t,(\widehat{y}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{y}^{j}\biggr]dt.\end{split}

We recall that
(
70
)
holds true for

m_{0}\in B_{N}(c)
. Next, we can take

m_{0}\in B_{N_{0}}(c)
, for some fixed
N_{0}
and then choose
N\geq N_{0}
, in which case
we indeed have
m_{0}\in B_{N}(c)
.
Now,
for a given threshold
\epsilon>0
(the reader should distinguish from
\varepsilon
), we can choose
N
and
\varepsilon
such that

\eta_{N,\varepsilon}<\epsilon/2
and then, by the second identity in
Proposition

4.11
,

\delta
(encoding the support of
\rho
) small enough such that the second term in the right-hand side is also less than
\epsilon/2
.
Therefore,
the left-hand side of
(
70
)
can be made smaller than
\epsilon
.
Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary,
we easily deduce that

V_{1}(0,m_{0})
and
V_{2}(0,m_{0})
must coincide.
This holds for any
m_{0}\in B_{N_{0}}(c)
. By an obvious density argument,
we obtain uniqueness.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

For a real
c\geq 1
, recall the notation
B_{N}(c)={\mathcal{P}}_{N}\cap\{m:\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla m(x)|\leq c,\ \inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}m(x)\geq 1/c\}

and let

C_{N}(c):=\{(\widehat{z}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}:\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}|k||\widehat{z}^{k}|<1/(2c)\}
,
with the usual convention that
\widehat{z}^{-k}=\overline{\widehat{z}^{k}}
.

For an element
m_{0}\in B_{N}(c)

and for

r=(\widehat{r}^{k})_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in C_{N}(c)
, define
m_{0}(r)
the element of
B_{N}(2c)

by

\widehat{m_{0}(r)}^{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widehat{m}_{0}^{k}+\widehat{r}^{k},&k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\},\\
0,&k\not\in F_{N},\end{array}\right.

and call
(m_{t}(r))_{0\leq t\leq T}
the solution of the non-linear Fokker-Planck equation
(
65
)
with
m_{0}(r)
as initial condition, for the same parameters
N
,
\varepsilon
and
\rho
as therein.

Then, we can find a constant
N_{c}
, only depending on
c
, on the strict convexity
property of
H
in the variable
p
and on the
d_{-2}
-Lipschitz and semi-concave properties of
V_{1}

and

V_{2}

in
(
65
),
and a constant
K
, independent of
\varepsilon
and
\rho
such that, if
N\geq N_{c}
,
for any

t\in[0,T]
,
the law of the mapping

r=\bigl(\widehat{r}^{k}\bigr)_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in C_{N}(c)\mapsto\Bigl(\widehat{m_{t}(r)}^{k}\Bigr)_{k\in F_{N}^{+}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}

has a density, bounded by
K
.

Proof.

The strategy of proof is quite clear but the implementation is demanding:
The whole point is to address the system of Fourier coefficients
satisfied by the solution of the Fokker-Planck equation
(
65
)
and to regard it as (a finite-dimensional) system of ODEs.
The solutions generates a flow of diffeomorphisms
and the key step is to provide a lower bound for the Jacobian that is independent of

t
,
\rho
and
\varepsilon
. This is the point where semi-concavity comes in explicitly.

Throughout the proof, we use the notation

V_{2-\lambda}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}:=\lambda V_{1}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}+(1-\lambda)V_{2}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}
, and similarly without the superscript
N,\varepsilon,\rho
.

First Step.

For an initial condition
m_{0}\in B_{N}(c)
and for

k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
we have

(71)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}\bigl(t,m_{t}\bigr)(x)e_{k}(x)\bigl(m_{t}*f_{N}\bigr)(x)dx.\end{split}

From
Proposition

3.14
, we now recall that, for every
t\in[0,T]
, for any

x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, any
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and any
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\begin{split}&\frac{\delta}{\delta m}V_{2-\lambda}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)\\
&=(1-\varepsilon)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\biggl\{\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\Bigl[\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{f}_{N}^{k}e_{k}(x)\Bigr]\biggl\}\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}.\end{split}

Therefore,

\begin{split}&\partial_{\mu}V_{2-\lambda}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)\\
&={\mathrm{i}}2\pi(1-\varepsilon)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\biggl\{\sum_{k\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}k\Bigl[\partial_{\widehat{m}^{k}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{f}_{N}^{k}e_{k}(x)\Bigr]\biggr\}\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}.\end{split}

In turn, recalling the notation
(
63
),
the last term in
(
71
)
becomes:

\begin{split}&{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m)(x)e_{k}(x)\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(x)dx\\
&={\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot\partial_{p}H\biggl(x,{\mathrm{i}}2\pi(1-\varepsilon)\\
&\hskip 30.0pt\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}\setminus\{0\}}\ell\Bigl[\partial_{\widehat{m}^{\ell}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\widehat{f}^{\ell}_{N}e_{\ell}(x)\Bigr]\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\\
&\hskip 200.0pt\times e_{k}(x)\bigl(m*f_{N}\bigr)(x)dx\,d\lambda.\end{split}

We recall that
we can interpret

\partial_{\widehat{m}^{\ell}}W(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N})
as the complex number
(see
(
30
)):

\begin{split}\partial_{\widehat{m}^{\ell}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)&=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\Re[\widehat{m}^{\ell}]}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\partial_{\Im[\widehat{m}^{\ell}]}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\\
&=\overline{\partial_{\widehat{m}^{-\ell}}W\bigl(t,m^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)}.\end{split}

Recalling that
\widehat{f}_{N}^{\ell}
is real, this allows us to rewrite
(
71
) in the form:

(72)

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{dt}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}\\
&\hskip 5.0pt-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot\partial_{p}H\biggl(x,-4\pi(1-\varepsilon)\\
&\hskip 30.0pt\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}^{+}}\ell\Im\Bigl[\partial_{\widehat{m}^{\ell}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)e_{\ell}(x)\Bigr]\widehat{f}^{\ell}_{N}\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\\
&\hskip 200.0pt\times e_{k}(x)\bigl(m_{t}*f_{N}\bigr)(x)\,dx\,d\lambda.\end{split}

By integration by parts, this
can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot\partial_{p}H\biggl(x,2\pi\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\times\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}^{+}}\biggl\{\ell\Bigl[\Im\bigl[e_{\ell}(x)\bigr]\partial_{\Re[\widehat{r}^{\ell}]}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{\ell}\bigr)-\Re\bigl[e_{\ell}(x)\bigr]\partial_{\Im[\widehat{r}^{\ell}]}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{\ell}\bigr)\bigr]\Bigr]\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}:j\not=\ell}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\biggr\}\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)

(73)

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt\times e_{k}(x)\bigl(m_{t}*f_{N}\bigr)(x)\,dx\,d\lambda.

\displaystyle=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt-2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot\partial_{p}H\biggl(x,-2\pi\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt\times\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}^{+}}\biggl\{\ell\Bigl[\Bigl(\Re\bigl[{\mathrm{i}}e_{\ell}(x)\bigr],\Im\bigl[{\mathrm{i}}e_{\ell}(x)\bigr]\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\Re[\widehat{r}^{\ell}],\Im[\widehat{r}^{\ell}]}\Bigl(\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]\biggr\}\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt\times{\mathrm{i}}e_{k}(x)\bigl(m_{t}*f_{N}\bigr)(x)\,dx\,d\lambda.

Notice that we passed the
{\mathrm{i}}
in front of the second line to
{\mathrm{i}}e_{k}(x)
inside the integral on the penultimate line.
Also, the symbol
\cdot
on the same penultimate line is used to denote the inner product in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Second Step.

We now compute the derivative of the flow
with respect to the real and imaginary parts of the
Fourier coefficients of the initial condition.
In order to avoid to repeat the computations twice, once for the derivative with respect to the real part and once for the derivative
with respect to the imaginary part,
we introduce the generic notation
\mathfrak{P}[z]
for a complex number
z
, which may stand for

\Re[z]

or

\Im[z]
.

Now, it makes sense to compute the derivative of the flow

\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}
, for

k,k^{\prime}\in F_{N}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{P}=\Re,\Im
.
By representing
m*f_{N}
in
the last line of
(
73
)
in the form

m*f_{N}(x)=1+2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in F_{N}^{+}}\Re\Bigl[\widehat{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}\widehat{f}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{N}e_{-\ell^{\prime}}(x)\Bigr],

we get, using
both
(
71
)
and
(
73
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigl(\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}\Bigr)=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.0pt+4\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}d\lambda\biggl(\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 7.0pt\times\biggl[\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in F_{N}^{+}}k\cdot\bigl(\partial^{2}_{pp}H(\lambda,t)\ell\bigr)\,\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}]}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\hat{f}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{N}\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widehat{m}_{t}^{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\times\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\Re\bigl[{\mathrm{i}}e_{\ell}(x)\bigr],\Im\bigl[{\mathrm{i}}e_{\ell}(x)\bigr]\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\Re[\widehat{r}^{\ell}],\Im[\widehat{r}^{\ell}]}\Bigl(\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]{\mathrm{i}}e_{k}(x)\bigl(m_{t}*f_{N}\bigr)(x)dx\biggr]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.0pt-4\pi\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}k\cdot D{\mathcal{H}}^{N,\varepsilon,\rho}(t,m_{t})(x){\mathrm{i}}e_{k}(x)\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in F_{N}^{+}}\Re\Bigl[\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widehat{m}_{t}^{\ell^{\prime}}\widehat{f}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{N}e_{-\ell^{\prime}}(x)\Bigr]\,dx,

with

\begin{split}\partial^{2}_{pp}H(\lambda,t)&=\partial^{2}_{pp}H\biggl(x,-2\pi\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\times\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}^{+}}\biggl\{\ell\Bigl[\Bigl(\Re\bigl[{\mathrm{i}}e_{\ell}(x)\bigr],\Im\bigl[{\mathrm{i}}e_{\ell}(x)\bigr]\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{\Re[\widehat{r}^{\ell}],\Im[\widehat{r}^{\ell}]}\Bigl(\prod_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}\rho\bigl(\widehat{r}^{j}\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]\biggr\}\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr).\end{split}

Expanding

\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}

as

(\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\Re[\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}],\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\Im[\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}])
,
we get a linear system of the form

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\Bigl(\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}[\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}]\Bigr)&=-2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}\bigl[\widehat{m}_{t}^{k}\bigr]\\
&\hskip 2.0pt+\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigl[A_{k,\ell}^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},\Re}(t)\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\Re[\widehat{m}_{t}^{\ell}]+A_{k,\ell}^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},\Im}(t)\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\Im[\widehat{m}_{t}^{\ell}]\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\sum_{\ell\in F_{N}^{+}}\Bigl[B_{k,\ell}^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},\Re}(t)\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\Re[\widehat{m}_{t}^{\ell}]+B_{k,\ell}^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},\Im}(t)\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}_{0}^{k^{\prime}}]}\Im[\widehat{m}_{t}^{\ell}]\Bigr],\end{split}

with

\begin{split}&A_{k,\ell}^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},\mathfrak{P}}(t)=\epsilon_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},{\mathfrak{P}}}4\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2|F_{N}^{+}|}}d\lambda\biggl(\bigotimes_{j\in F_{N}^{+}}d\widehat{r}^{j}\biggr)\\
&\hskip 7.0pt\times\biggl[\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in F_{N}^{+}}k\cdot\bigl(\partial^{2}_{pp}H(\lambda,t)\ell^{\prime}\bigr)\,\partial_{\mathfrak{P}[\widehat{m}^{\ell}]}V_{2-\lambda}\bigl(t,m_{t}^{N,\varepsilon}(r)*f_{N}\bigr)\hat{f}^{\ell}_{N}\\
